We actually went to Switzerland for our honeymoon, to this little town called Zermatt. You can't even drive cars there, it's all electric vehicles and horse-drawn carriages. We took this train up a mountain to see the Matterhorn, and the air was so thin I felt dizzy the whole time. We had fondue at this restaurant at the top, and I dropped my piece of bread in the cheese pot. Apparently, there's a tradition that if a woman drops her bread in the fondue, she has to kiss the man next to her, which was fine since it was my husband, but if it had been some stranger... \n\nOh, sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? He took it with us when we went to his sister's wedding in upstate New York last year. Everyone had to grab their folding chairs and sprint for the reception tent. My heels kept sinking into the mud, and I completely ruined a brand new pair of suede pumps. Just that feeling of not being in my body and everything looking fake when I'm walking around campus.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant problems in your daily life, such as at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it really has been messing things up. I just haven't been able to do my job normally at all. Last week, I actually had to just get up and walk out right in the middle of a planning meeting. And I've already missed two of my mentoring sessions with my undergrads, which I feel terrible about. I'm also completely avoiding certain parts of campus that I'm supposed to use for my department. \n\nIt's been so hard because I usually love working with the undergrads. Some of them are just so bright and eager. There's this one student, Chloe, she's doing her senior thesis on mid-century modernist literature, and she found this incredible collection of unpublished letters. We were supposed to go over her first draft on Tuesday, but I just couldn't make myself go to the building. She wants to apply to grad school next year, maybe somewhere on the East Coast. I went to grad school in Boston, actually. The winters there were absolutely brutal. I remember one year it snowed so much that they ran out of places to put the snow, so they had these massive dump trucks hauling it away and melting it in these giant machines by the harbor. I had this awful apartment in Somerville where the radiator clanked all night long. It sounded like someone hitting a metal pipe with a wrench every twenty minutes. I tried wearing earplugs, but then I couldn't hear my alarm clock in the morning, and I ended up being late for my seminar on James Joyce. Have you ever tried to read *Ulysses*? It's just... well, people say it's brilliant, but honestly, half the time I had no idea what was going on...\n\nBut, um, anyway. Yes. It's definitely causing problems. Missing meetings, skipping my mentoring sessions, avoiding parts of campus... I'm just not doing my job the way I'm supposed to right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience where you felt like you lost touch with reality, even if it was just for a short time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not before this whole thing happened. I mean, I've never had anything like that where I lost touch with reality. I've always been a very grounded person, you know? Very practical. My husband always says I'm the one who keeps our feet on the ground because he's a bit of a dreamer. He's an architect, actually, and he's always coming up with these wild, impossible designs for houses that would cost millions of dollars to build. Last month he drew up these plans for a house that was entirely cantilevered over a ravine. It looked like a spaceship balancing on a toothpick. I asked him how anyone would even get groceries into the kitchen, and he just waved his hand and said something about a funicular. A funicular! Like we're living in the Swiss Alps or something. We actually went to Switzerland for our honeymoon, to this little town called Zermatt. You can't even drive cars there, it's all electric vehicles and horse-drawn carriages. We took this train up a mountain to see the Matterhorn, and the air was so thin I felt dizzy the whole time. We had fondue at this restaurant at the top, and I dropped my piece of bread in the cheese pot. Apparently, there's a tradition that if a woman drops her bread in the fondue, she has to kiss the man next to her, which was fine since it was my husband, but if it had been some stranger... \n\nOh, sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, to answer your question, before the accident with the lighting rig, I've never experienced anything where I felt disconnected from reality or anything like that. Never.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where this feeling of detachment or being behind a pane of glass has completely gone away, even if only for a short time?\n\nPatient: Well, sometimes it fades a little when I'm really distracted, but... honestly, like I said before, it's what scares me the most about all this. When I'm just walking across campus, it's like I'm watching everything through glass. Like I'm not fully in my own body, and the buildings and the students around me just look... slightly unreal. \n\nIt's so unsettling, especially near the old brick buildings by the quad. They have this ivy growing all up the sides, and in the fall it turns this brilliant, fiery red. I always try to take a picture of it, but the colors never look right on my phone. My husband says I need to adjust the white balance, but honestly, I don't even know where that setting is. He has this massive DSLR camera with all these interchangeable lenses. He took it with us when we went to his sister's wedding in upstate New York last year. The wedding was entirely outdoors, in this apple orchard, which sounded lovely until it started pouring rain right as she was walking down the aisle. Everyone had to grab their folding chairs and sprint for the reception tent. My heels kept sinking into the mud, and I completely ruined a brand new pair of suede pumps. You can never really get mud out of suede, can you? I tried that little wire brush thing, but it just made the nap look all scuffed and weird. I ended up having to throw them away, which broke my heart because I found them on sale at Nordstrom Rack for like, sixty percent off...\n\nBut, um... sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, right, the glass feeling. Yeah, it's just... it's terrifying when it hits me on campus, feeling like I'm not in my body and everything is fake.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of feeling detached and like you're watching everything through a pane of glass lasted for more than a few days but less than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, it started exactly six days ago. That's when the lighting rig collapsed during the showcase, and I've felt this way ever since. Six days. It feels like a lot longer, honestly, but I know it was Tuesday because I always wear my lucky green scarf on showcase days. I knitted it myself, actually. It took me three months because I kept dropping stitches and having to unravel whole rows. Knitting is supposed to be relaxing, right? That's what everyone says. My grandmother used to knit these incredible, complicated Aran sweaters with all the cables and bobbles. She could do it while watching television, not even looking at her hands. She used to watch those old soap operas, the ones that were on in the middle of the day. *Days of Our Lives*, I think? Or maybe *General Hospital*. She had this huge crush on one of the actors, I can't remember his name, but he had this very dramatic silver hair and always played the villain. She had a framed picture of him on her nightstand right next to a picture of my grandfather, which I always thought was a little strange, but my grandfather just thought it was funny. He was a very patient man. He used to fix antique clocks in his basement... \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, the duration. Yes, it's been six days since the rig came down. Just six days.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of feeling detached and like you're watching everything through a pane of glass lasted for less than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was just saying, it's been exactly six days. Scott Fitzgerald was really going for a superhero origin story. Although, I guess Gatsby did have a secret identity, in a way. My husband loves those superhero movies. It's a '67, I think? Or maybe a '68. My dad used to have a classic car when I was growing up, a Chevy Nova, and we'd spend hours on the weekends just polishing the chrome. We just had a Slip 'N Slide in the backyard, and one time my brother went down it too fast and slid right into the neighbor's fence. He broke his collarbone and had to wear this awful brace for six weeks right in the middle of summer. It was so hot that year, like, record-breaking heat. We practically lived at the community pool, and they had this snack bar that sold those frozen push-up pops... \n\nBut, sorry. Yeah, I just stayed home instead of going to the party. I haven't really been going anywhere or seeing anyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel sad, exactly. Or empty. Just... I don't know, maybe just not really feeling much of anything? I guess I've just been kind of numb. But you know, speaking of feeling empty, my refrigerator has been completely bare for like three days. I keep meaning to go to the grocery store, but the one near my house is always so crowded. It's a Trader Joe's, and the parking lot is just a nightmare. I swear they make those spaces smaller on purpose. Last time I was there, this guy in a giant SUV parked so close to me I had to climb in through the passenger side. And I spilled my coffee all over the center console doing it. It was that pumpkin spice latte they only have in the fall, which is my favorite. My husband hates the smell of pumpkin spice, though. He says it smells like a craft store. We used to go to this craft store when I was little, Michaels, I think? My mom was really into scrapbooking for a few years, and she had this whole room dedicated to it with all these special scissors that cut in different patterns...\n\nBut, um, anyway. No, I don't think I've been feeling sad or empty. Just kind of... there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried about things lately?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've been feeling anxious, really. I mean, not more than usual, I guess? I haven't really noticed feeling worried about anything specific. Actually, the only thing I was worried about recently was my cat, Barnaby. He's this big orange tabby, and he swallowed a piece of string last week. I had to take him to the emergency vet at like two in the morning. The waiting room was freezing, and they had this TV playing a nature documentary about penguins on a loop. Did you know emperor penguins can hold their breath for over 20 minutes? It's wild. My husband loves penguins. We went to the aquarium for our anniversary a couple of years ago, and they had this whole exhibit where you could actually feed them. The fish smelled terrible, though, like really strong sardines. I can't stand the smell of fish. My dad used to make tuna casseroles every Friday during Lent when I was growing up, and the whole house would just reek of it for days. He always used those crushed potato chips on top, which I guess was the best part...\n\nBut, um, sorry. Anxious? No, I don't really think I've been feeling anxious or worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't... I haven't really felt guilty about anything. I mean, not that I can think of. Well, maybe a little guilty that I haven't been helping out the lead teacher as much at work, but I haven't even been going in. I'm a teaching assistant for second graders, and it's usually so busy this time of year. They're doing their unit on the solar system right now. The kids get so into it, making these little papier-mâché planets. Last year, this one little boy, Leo, painted his Jupiter bright neon green because it was his favorite color. It was actually really cute. His mom is a florist, and she brought in these huge bouquets for Teacher Appreciation Week. They had these big pink lilies in them. I love lilies, but you have to be so careful with them if you have cats, you know? Like with Barnaby. The pollen is super toxic to them. I read this article once about a woman who almost lost her cat because he just brushed up against a lily and then licked his fur. It's crazy how something so pretty can be so dangerous. Kind of like oleander, I think? They plant that all over the highways in California... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. No, I haven't really been feeling guilty. Just kind of staying out of everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: My thoughts? Like someone controlling them? No, definitely not. I mean, my thoughts are my own. They might be a bit... well, they're mine. I haven't had anything weird like that. Although, speaking of minds being controlled, did you ever see that movie with the aliens that take over people's bodies? The Invasion of the Body Snatchers, I think it was called. My husband made me watch the old black-and-white version a few months ago. He's really into classic sci-fi. He collects these vintage movie posters, and he has them all framed in the basement. The basement used to just be this dark, damp storage space, but he spent all last summer finishing it. He put down this laminate flooring that looks exactly like real hardwood, but it's supposed to be waterproof. Which is good, because our washing machine leaked everywhere right after we moved in. It was a disaster. The water soaked through the drywall, and we had to run these giant industrial fans for a week just to dry it out. The fans were so loud, it sounded like an airplane was taking off in the house. I couldn't even hear myself think, which, you know, going back to what you were saying...\n\nBut, um, no. No one is controlling my mind or my thoughts. Just me in here.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by feeling like you're watching everything through a pane of glass, like you're not fully in your own body?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... honestly, it's what scares me the most right now. When I walk across campus, it just feels exactly like that, like I’m watching everything through glass. Like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and the students around me are just... slightly unreal. \n\nSpeaking of the students, they all wear those massive backpacks now that look like they're going on a three-day hiking trip. I saw this one girl the other day carrying a backpack that was literally half her size, and it had all these little enamel pins all over the front of it. One of them was a little cartoon avocado with a smiley face. Avocados are getting so expensive right now, have you noticed? I went to the grocery store on Tuesday to get stuff for taco night, and they were almost three dollars for just one. And you can never even tell if they're actually good inside until you cut them open. The last time I bought one, it was completely brown and stringy inside. I hate wasting food. My mom was super strict about us cleaning our plates when I was a kid. We had meatloaf every Wednesday, and if you didn't finish it, it was packed in your lunchbox on Thursday. I still can't even look at meatloaf without feeling a little nauseous. She used to put this weird ketchup glaze on top that would get all crusty...\n\nBut, um, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, the glass thing. Yeah, it just feels like the buildings and everything are slightly unreal, like I'm just observing it all from somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like you're watching everything through a pane of glass?\n\nPatient: It's been... well, it started exactly six days ago. Right after the lighting rig collapsed during the showcase. That whole thing was just... I mean, the noise alone was terrifying. But yeah, for the last six days, it's been like this. \n\nThe showcase is usually the highlight of the year, you know? The second graders had been practicing this little song about the planets for weeks. They were all lined up on the risers, and we had made these little star headbands for them to wear out of that glittery cardstock you can get at the craft store. I actually got a paper cut from that cardstock, right on the webbing between my thumb and pointer finger. Paper cuts there are the worst, aren't they? Every time you move your hand, it stings. I tried to put a band-aid on it, but it wouldn't stick right because of the lotion I use. I have to use this special unscented oatmeal lotion because my skin gets so dry in the winter. My dermatologist recommended it. She's this really nice lady with the most amazing collection of vintage glasses frames. I swear, every time I go in, she has a different pair on. Last time, they were these bright red cat-eye ones that looked straight out of the 1950s. I could never pull off something like that. My face is too round, I think. I tried on a pair of oversized sunglasses once at the mall, and my husband said I looked like a bug...\n\nBut, um, what did you ask? Oh, right, how long. Yeah, just since the rig fell. Six days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're spaced out or detached from yourself most of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I do feel spaced out a lot. Like I said before, it's what scares me the most right now. When I'm just walking across campus, it hits me. It feels exactly like I'm watching everything through a pane of glass. Like I'm not really inside my own body, you know? And the buildings, the students walking by... everything just looks slightly unreal. \n\nSpeaking of the buildings, they're doing all this construction on the science center right now. They've got these massive cranes out there. I always wonder how the people who drive those cranes get all the way up into that little box at the top. Do they have to climb a ladder on the inside, or is there some kind of elevator? It seems so dangerous, especially when it's windy. The wind here has been crazy lately. It blew my neighbor's trash can all the way down the street on garbage day. It was one of those big blue recycling bins, and all their cardboard boxes were just blowing everywhere. I went out to help them pick it up, and they had so many Amazon boxes. I mean, I order from Amazon too, mostly just for things I can't find at the store, like this specific kind of green tea I like. It has jasmine in it, and it comes in these little silk pyramid bags instead of the paper ones. The paper ones always rip when I try to take the tag off, and then you get little tea leaves floating in your mug, which just ruins the whole cup. I usually drink it out of this mug my sister got me that says \"World's Okayest Sister.\" She thinks she's hilarious. She lives in Ohio now, so I don't see her as much, but... \n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? But yeah, to answer your question, I do feel detached. Just... walking around campus, feeling like I'm behind glass and not really in my body. It's really unsettling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intense emotional reactions to certain things, like the sound of the collapsed lighting rig or anything else that might have triggered this feeling of detachment?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. It's... it's the sound mostly. The sound of that rig coming down just keeps barging into my head out of nowhere. Like, I'll just be sitting at my kitchen island grading papers or making coffee, and suddenly it's there. \n\nI've been making a lot of coffee at home lately, actually. I had to switch to decaf in the afternoons, though. I buy those whole beans from that little local roaster downtown—the one with the dark green awning? They have this Ethiopian blend that has these really nice blueberry notes to it. It sounds weird, blueberry in coffee, but it's actually delicious. You have to grind it right before you brew it, though, or it loses all the flavor. My coffee grinder is so incredibly loud, which my cat absolutely hates. He's a rescue, a big orange tabby named Barnaby, and he's terrified of loud noises. Every time I turn the grinder on, he scrambles under the sofa and won't come out for at least an hour. I tried buying him one of those calming pheromone plug-ins from the pet store, but I don't think it did anything at all. It just made the living room smell faintly like wet laundry, and those refills are ridiculously expensive. I think they just slap a picture of a sleeping cat on the box and charge double for it...\n\nOh, gosh, sorry. I'm completely off track again, aren't I? What I meant was, when that sound barges into my head while I'm just trying to grade or make my coffee, the reaction is really intense. It's like I can suddenly see the volunteer on the floor and all the students pushing forward, just... exactly like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're watching everything through a pane of glass all the time, or are there moments when it goes away?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's not exactly all the time, but it happens a lot. Like I mentioned before, it's what really scares me the most about all this. When I'm just walking across campus, it hits me. It feels like I'm watching everything through a pane of glass, like I'm not fully in my own body. And the buildings, the students walking by... they all just seem slightly unreal. \n\nIt’s especially weird when I'm walking past the library. They have those huge glass windows on the front, and they just washed them last week. I saw the guys out there with those long squeegees on poles. I always wonder how they don't leave streaks. Whenever I wash my windows at home, no matter what I use—vinegar, newspaper, that blue spray stuff—there are always streaks when the sun hits it at a certain angle. My mom used to swear by using coffee filters to dry the glass, but honestly, who has that many coffee filters just lying around? Plus, I use a French press on the weekends, so I don't even buy filters anymore. The French press makes such a richer cup of coffee, don't you think? Though it is a pain to clean the grounds out of the bottom. You can't just rinse them down the sink or it clogs the disposal. Our disposal broke last Thanksgiving right before we were supposed to have twelve people over for dinner. My husband had to take the whole pipe apart under the sink, and it smelled absolutely awful...\n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the glass feeling. Yeah, it's not every single second, but it happens enough that it's really terrifying. Just that feeling of not being in my body and everything looking fake when I'm walking around campus.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant problems in your daily life, such as at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it really has been messing things up. I just haven't been able to do my job normally at all. Last week, I actually had to just get up and walk out right in the middle of a planning meeting. And I've already missed two of my mentoring sessions with my undergrads, which I feel terrible about. I'm also completely avoiding certain parts of campus that I'm supposed to use for my department. \n\nIt's been so hard because I usually love working with the undergrads. Some of them are just so bright and eager. There's this one student, Chloe, she's doing her senior thesis on mid-century modernist literature, and she found this incredible collection of unpublished letters. We were supposed to go over her first draft on Tuesday, but I just couldn't make myself go to the building. She wants to apply to grad school next year, maybe somewhere on the East Coast. I went to grad school in Boston, actually. The winters there were absolutely brutal. I remember one year it snowed so much that they ran out of places to put the snow, so they had these massive dump trucks hauling it away and melting it in these giant machines by the harbor. I had this awful apartment in Somerville where the radiator clanked all night long. It sounded like someone hitting a metal pipe with a wrench every twenty minutes. I tried wearing earplugs, but then I couldn't hear my alarm clock in the morning, and I ended up being late for my seminar on James Joyce. Have you ever tried to read *Ulysses*? It's just... well, people say it's brilliant, but honestly, half the time I had no idea what was going on...\n\nBut, um, anyway. Yes. It's definitely causing problems. Missing meetings, skipping my mentoring sessions, avoiding parts of campus... I'm just not doing my job the way I'm supposed to right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience where you felt like you lost touch with reality, even if it was just for a short time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not before this whole thing happened. I mean, I've never had anything like that where I lost touch with reality. I've always been a very grounded person, you know? Very practical. My husband always says I'm the one who keeps our feet on the ground because he's a bit of a dreamer. He's an architect, actually, and he's always coming up with these wild, impossible designs for houses that would cost millions of dollars to build. Last month he drew up these plans for a house that was entirely cantilevered over a ravine. It looked like a spaceship balancing on a toothpick. I asked him how anyone would even get groceries into the kitchen, and he just waved his hand and said something about a funicular. A funicular! Like we're living in the Swiss Alps or something. We actually went to Switzerland for our honeymoon, to this little town called Zermatt. You can't even drive cars there, it's all electric vehicles and horse-drawn carriages. We took this train up a mountain to see the Matterhorn, and the air was so thin I felt dizzy the whole time. We had fondue at this restaurant at the top, and I dropped my piece of bread in the cheese pot. Apparently, there's a tradition that if a woman drops her bread in the fondue, she has to kiss the man next to her, which was fine since it was my husband, but if it had been some stranger... \n\nOh, sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, to answer your question, before the accident with the lighting rig, I've never experienced anything where I felt disconnected from reality or anything like that. Never.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where this feeling of detachment or being behind a pane of glass has completely gone away, even if only for a short time?\n\nPatient: Well, sometimes it fades a little when I'm really distracted, but... honestly, like I said before, it's what scares me the most about all this. When I'm just walking across campus, it's like I'm watching everything through glass. Like I'm not fully in my own body, and the buildings and the students around me just look... slightly unreal. \n\nIt's so unsettling, especially near the old brick buildings by the quad. They have this ivy growing all up the sides, and in the fall it turns this brilliant, fiery red. I always try to take a picture of it, but the colors never look right on my phone. My husband says I need to adjust the white balance, but honestly, I don't even know where that setting is. He has this massive DSLR camera with all these interchangeable lenses. He took it with us when we went to his sister's wedding in upstate New York last year. The wedding was entirely outdoors, in this apple orchard, which sounded lovely until it started pouring rain right as she was walking down the aisle. Everyone had to grab their folding chairs and sprint for the reception tent. My heels kept sinking into the mud, and I completely ruined a brand new pair of suede pumps. You can never really get mud out of suede, can you? I tried that little wire brush thing, but it just made the nap look all scuffed and weird. I ended up having to throw them away, which broke my heart because I found them on sale at Nordstrom Rack for like, sixty percent off...\n\nBut, um... sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, right, the glass feeling. Yeah, it's just... it's terrifying when it hits me on campus, feeling like I'm not in my body and everything is fake.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of feeling detached and like you're watching everything through a pane of glass lasted for more than a few days but less than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, it started exactly six days ago. That's when the lighting rig collapsed during the showcase, and I've felt this way ever since. Six days. It feels like a lot longer, honestly, but I know it was Tuesday because I always wear my lucky green scarf on showcase days. I knitted it myself, actually. It took me three months because I kept dropping stitches and having to unravel whole rows. Knitting is supposed to be relaxing, right? That's what everyone says. My grandmother used to knit these incredible, complicated Aran sweaters with all the cables and bobbles. She could do it while watching television, not even looking at her hands. She used to watch those old soap operas, the ones that were on in the middle of the day. *Days of Our Lives*, I think? Or maybe *General Hospital*. She had this huge crush on one of the actors, I can't remember his name, but he had this very dramatic silver hair and always played the villain. She had a framed picture of him on her nightstand right next to a picture of my grandfather, which I always thought was a little strange, but my grandfather just thought it was funny. He was a very patient man. He used to fix antique clocks in his basement... \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, the duration. Yes, it's been six days since the rig came down. Just six days.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of feeling detached and like you're watching everything through a pane of glass lasted for less than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was just saying, it's been exactly six days. Since the showcase, when the rig fell. So definitely less than a month. It feels like a month, though. Time has been dragging so slowly since Tuesday. Usually, my weeks just fly by, especially during midterms when I have a stack of freshman essays to grade. They're always so full of themselves, the freshmen, writing these sweeping statements about the human condition when they're, what, eighteen? Nineteen? I had one student last semester who wrote a whole paper comparing *The Great Gatsby* to a Marvel movie. I mean, I appreciate the creativity, but I don't think F. Scott Fitzgerald was really going for a superhero origin story. Although, I guess Gatsby did have a secret identity, in a way. My husband loves those superhero movies. We went to see the latest Batman one, and it was almost three hours long. Three hours! I had to get up to use the restroom twice, and the theater was so dark I almost tripped on the stairs. They really should put better lighting on those steps, it's a huge liability. Imagine if someone fell and broke their ankle, they could sue the theater for millions... \n\nOh, sorry. But yes, to answer your question, it hasn't been a month. Just the six days since the accident.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or traumatic events besides the lighting rig collapse in the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. It's really just been the rig collapsing, that's the only major thing. My life is usually pretty quiet, honestly. Just grading papers, going to classes, trying to keep up with the reading list. I'm taking this seminar on Victorian literature this semester, and the reading load is just astronomical. We had to read *Middlemarch* in two weeks. Have you ever seen a copy of *Middlemarch*? It's like a brick. You could literally use it as a doorstop. My cat, Barnaby, actually loves sleeping on my textbooks when I leave them open on the desk. He's a Maine Coon mix, so he's massive, and he just sprawls right across the pages. He sheds everywhere, too. I have to keep one of those sticky lint rollers in every room of the apartment, and even then, I always find orange fur on my black slacks when I get to campus. I tried getting him one of those special deshedding brushes, but he hates it. The second he sees it, he runs under the sofa and won't come out for hours. We had to coax him out with tuna water last time. He's very food-motivated, which I guess I can relate to. I've been craving those little powdered donuts lately, the ones that come in the crinkly bags at the gas station...\n\nUm, but no. No other big stressors or traumas or anything. Just the accident on Tuesday."
